Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Langflow file-ingestion endpoint receives archive/bundle upload (CVE-2026-55447 delivery)
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Langflow worker reads secret_key / credential files via symlink TAR traversal (CVE-2026-55447) · [LLM] Langflow Python worker spawns shell / recon child process (CVE-2026-55447 Python Interpreter RCE)
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → Article-specific behavioural hunt — [GHSA / CRITICAL] CVE-2026-55447: Langflow: BaseFileComponent-based nodes arbitr
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Langflow file-ingestion endpoint receives archive/bundle upload (CVE-2026-55447 delivery) AIDelivery Low
Hunts POST uploads to Langflow's file-ingestion API (/api/v1/files/upload, /api/v2/files) that feed BaseFileComponent nodes (Read File, Docling, Unstructured, etc.). CVE-2026-55447 is delivered by uploading a TAR bundle whose symlink members point at arbitrary absolute paths, so the upload itself is the first observable step. Reverse-proxy/WAF logs are the only place this inbound request is visible. Rationale: Uses the concrete Langflow upload endpoints (/api/v1/files/upload, /api/v2/files) from the docs the advisory references; these feed the BaseFileComponent nodes the article lists. Low fidelity alone (legit RAG document uploads look identical at the proxy layer with no body inspection), so it is a delivery-phase pivot to correlate with the host-side UCs. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/langflow/CVE-2026-55447/ • https://www.ibm.com/support/pages/node/7273426 • https://docs.langflow.org/api-files
ATT&CKT1190 T1659
Data sourcesWeb
Expected false positives · estimated rate: high — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate users uploading documents/datasets into RAG ingestion flows
  • Automated/scheduled ingestion pipelines or ETL jobs seeding vector stores
  • CI/CD or demo seeding of flows via the files API
  • Bulk dataset migration uploads by data engineers
  • Synthetic monitoring / health-check probes hitting the files endpoint
Suppression filter
| where DestinationServiceName !has "healthcheck" and SourceIP !in ("127.0.0.1")
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the SourceIP, user-agent and authenticated principal for the upload; confirm whether the source is an expected internal ingestion service or an unknown/external client. 2) Contain: if the source is untrusted, block it at the WAF/reverse proxy and pivot to the Langflow host to check whether the host-side symlink/sensitive-file-read UC fired in the same window; if confirmed, isolate the host. 3) Hunt: on the Langflow host, look for the worker reading secret_key/etc/shadow/.ssh/.aws (UC2) and the Python worker spawning shells (UC3) within minutes of this upload; rotate LANGFLOW_SECRET_KEY and any exposed credentials and confirm the instance is >= 1.9.2.
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where RequestMethod =~ "POST"
| where RequestURL has_any ("/api/v1/files/upload", "/api/v2/files", "/api/v1/files")
| summarize Uploads = count(), Paths = make_set(RequestURL, 20), FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated), UserAgents = make_set(RequestClientApplication, 10) by SourceIP, DestinationHostName, DestinationServiceName
| order by Uploads desc

Langflow worker reads secret_key / credential files via symlink TAR traversal (CVE-2026-55447) AIExploitation High
Hunts the Langflow Python worker (python/uvicorn/gunicorn running langflow) reading sensitive files outside its data directory — secret_key, /etc/shadow, /etc/passwd, SSH/AWS keys, private_key.pem. CVE-2026-55447's _unpack_bundle follows symlinks inside an uploaded TAR, so the worker opens whatever absolute path the symlink targets and pipes it into the vector store. A web worker reading /etc/shadow or an SSH/AWS key is highly abnormal. Rationale: Anchors on the article's own mechanics: _unpack_bundle in base_file.py following a TAR symlink to read Langflow's secret_key (JWT signing secret). secret_key, private_key.pem and config-dir paths confirmed via Langflow docs/DeepWiki; /etc/shadow, SSH and AWS keys are the article's 'any file on the file-system by absolute path' generalisation. A web worker reading these is high-fidelity. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/langflow/CVE-2026-55447/ • https://www.ibm.com/support/pages/node/7273426 • https://deepwiki.com/langflow-ai/langflow/4.9-file-management-and-storage
ATT&CKT1552.001 T1083 T1190
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Langflow legitimately reading its OWN secret_key/private_key.pem from the canonical config dir at process start (filter on the symlink/uploads path or exclude boot window)
  • Configuration-management runs (Ansible/Chef) reading /etc as the langflow service user
  • Backup agents snapshotting home dirs containing .ssh/.aws
  • Vulnerability/file-integrity scanners enumerating /etc and key files
  • Admin troubleshooting cat-ing /etc/passwd in the container
Suppression filter
| where not (TargetFilePath endswith "/.cache/langflow/secret_key" or TargetFilePath endswith "/app/langflow/secret_key") or EventType !in ("FileAccessed")
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the TargetFilePath — reads of /etc/shadow, ~/.ssh or ~/.aws by a langflow worker are immediately suspicious; a secret_key read is suspicious mainly when it comes from the uploads/component temp path rather than the canonical config dir. Correlate with a recent files-API upload (UC1). 2) Contain: isolate the host, revoke the Langflow session, and rotate LANGFLOW_SECRET_KEY plus any SSH/AWS credentials that were readable — assume the JWT signing secret is compromised. 3) Hunt: search for forged-JWT-authenticated flow creation and the Python worker spawning shells (UC3); verify the Langflow build and upgrade to >= 1.9.2 (PR #12945).
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventType in ("FileAccessed", "FileRead", "FileOpened")
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("python", "uvicorn", "gunicorn") or ActingProcessCommandLine has "langflow"
| where TargetFilePath has_any ("/etc/shadow", "/etc/passwd", "/.ssh/", "id_rsa", "/.aws/credentials", "secret_key", "private_key.pem")
| where not(ActingProcessName has_any ("apt", "dpkg", "yum", "sshd", "cron", "systemd"))
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetFilePath, EventType
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Langflow Worker Reads Credential/Secret Files (CVE-2026-55447 symlink traversal)
id: a4c9d2e1-8b73-4f50-9a6c-1e2f3b4c5d60
status: experimental
description: Langflow Python worker opening secret_key, /etc/shadow, SSH or AWS credential files — the arbitrary-file-read payoff of CVE-2026-55447 symlink-in-TAR extraction.
references:
  - https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-ccv6-r384-xp75
  - https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/langflow/CVE-2026-55447/
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/06/21
tags:
  - attack.t1552.001
  - attack.t1083
  - attack.credential_access
logsource:
  category: file_access
  product: linux
detection:
  selection_proc:
    Image|endswith:
      - '/python'
      - '/python3'
      - '/uvicorn'
      - '/gunicorn'
  selection_target:
    TargetFilename|contains:
      - '/etc/shadow'
      - '/etc/passwd'
      - '/.ssh/'
      - 'id_rsa'
      - '/.aws/credentials'
      - 'secret_key'
      - 'private_key.pem'
  condition: selection_proc and selection_target
level: high
#event_simpleName=FileOpenInfo
| ContextImageFileName=/\/(python[0-9.]*|uvicorn|gunicorn)$/i
| TargetFileName=/(\/etc\/shadow|\/etc\/passwd|\/\.ssh\/|id_rsa|\/\.aws\/credentials|secret_key|private_key\.pem)/i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ContextImageFileName, TargetFileName], function=count())
| count > 0

| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)

Langflow Python worker spawns shell / recon child process (CVE-2026-55447 Python Interpreter RCE) AIExploitation Medium
Hunts the Langflow web worker (python/uvicorn/gunicorn with 'langflow' in its command line) spawning shells or recon/network tooling (sh, bash, curl, wget, nc, whoami, id, base64, crontab). After stealing secret_key and forging a JWT, CVE-2026-55447 escalates to RCE by creating a flow with the 'Python Interpreter' node and running arbitrary Python — which surfaces as the long-lived server process spawning unexpected children. Rationale: Targets the article's explicit RCE primitive: 'create a new flow with Python Interpreter node, fill it with arbitrary Python code and execute it.' The Langflow server is a long-lived python/uvicorn/gunicorn process; it spawning /bin/sh or curl/wget/nc is the observable shape of code execution through that node. Parent scoped to 'langflow' in the cmdline keeps it specific. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/langflow/CVE-2026-55447/ • https://www.ibm.com/support/pages/node/7273426
ATT&CKT1059.006 T1059.004 T1190
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate data-science use of the Python Interpreter node that shells out (subprocess/os.system) for tooling
  • Langflow components that legitimately invoke ffmpeg/curl/git (e.g. Video File, web loaders)
  • Runtime pip/package installs spawning sh during component setup
  • Container entrypoint or healthcheck scripts run under the same python parent
  • Notebook/REPL experimentation by developers on a non-prod Langflow
Suppression filter
| where not (ProcessCommandLine has_any ("pip install", "pip3 install", "/healthcheck", "ffmpeg"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: inspect the child ProcessCommandLine — recon (whoami/id/uname), egress (curl/wget/nc) or chmod/crontab from a langflow worker are strong RCE signals; benign data-science shell-outs usually reference project tooling. Check whether the org even permits the Python Interpreter node. 2) Contain: isolate the host, kill the worker, and revoke active Langflow sessions; if UC2 also fired, treat the JWT secret as compromised and rotate it. 3) Hunt: trace the child process tree for persistence (cron, SSH key writes, new users) and outbound C2; review files-API upload logs (UC1) and sensitive-file reads (UC2) on the same host to confirm the full chain, then upgrade to >= 1.9.2.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName has_any ("python", "python3", "uvicorn", "gunicorn")
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "langflow" or InitiatingProcessFolderPath has "langflow"
| where FileName in~ ("sh", "bash", "dash", "zsh", "curl", "wget", "nc", "ncat", "netcat", "whoami", "id", "uname", "hostname", "base64", "chmod", "crontab")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, FolderPath, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("python", "uvicorn", "gunicorn")
| where ParentProcessCommandLine has "langflow"
| where TargetProcessName has_any ("bash", "dash", "zsh", "curl", "wget", "ncat", "netcat", "whoami", "uname", "hostname", "base64", "crontab") or TargetProcessName endswith "/sh" or TargetProcessName endswith "/nc" or TargetProcessName endswith "/id"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine, TargetProcessName, TargetProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Langflow Python Worker Spawns Shell or Recon Tool (CVE-2026-55447 Python Interpreter RCE)
id: c3f5a8b2-1d49-4e67-8a0b-2c4d6e8f9a11
status: experimental
description: Langflow web worker spawning a shell or recon/network binary — the RCE stage of CVE-2026-55447 via the Python Interpreter node after JWT forgery.
references:
  - https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-ccv6-r384-xp75

author: SOC Detection Engineering

tags:
  - attack.t1059.006
  - attack.t1059.004

  category: process_creation
  product: linux
detection:
  selection_parent:
    ParentImage|endswith:
      - '/python'
      - '/python3'
      - '/uvicorn'
      - '/gunicorn'
  selection_child:
    Image|endswith:
      - '/sh'
      - '/bash'
      - '/dash'
      - '/zsh'
      - '/curl'
      - '/wget'
      - '/nc'
      - '/ncat'
      - '/whoami'
      - '/id'
      - '/uname'
      - '/base64'
      - '/chmod'
      - '/crontab'
  condition: selection_parent and selection_child
level: high
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2
| ParentBaseFileName=/^(python[0-9.]*|uvicorn|gunicorn)$/i
| ImageFileName=/\/(sh|bash|dash|zsh|curl|wget|nc|ncat|netcat|whoami|id|uname|hostname|base64|chmod|crontab)$/i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ParentBaseFileName, ImageFileName, CommandLine], function=count())
| count > 0

ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-55447")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-55447")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → [LLM] Langflow IDOR enumeration — single source fan-out to /api/v1/responses (CVE-2026-55255) · [LLM] Asset exposure — Langflow instances vulnerable to CVE-2026-55255 (<1.9.1) · Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)

Langflow IDOR endpoint access — POST to /api/v1/responses (CVE-2026-55255) AIExploitation Medium
Surfaces POST traffic to the Langflow OpenAI-compatible endpoints affected by CVE-2026-55255 (/api/v1/responses, /api/v2/workflow, /api/v1/run*). An authenticated attacker abuses the IDOR by passing a victim's flow UUID in the request body; web access logs reveal which authenticated principals and source IPs are reaching these endpoints and whether the server returned 200 (flow executed). Rationale: Uses the exact affected routes named in the advisory (/api/v1/responses, plus /api/v2/workflow and /api/v1/run* that the fix also hardened) and the POST method from the PoC curl. Defender/Falcon/Datadog left empty because EDR/FDR telemetry cannot see the HTTP request path/body of a self-hosted Langflow app — this detection lives in web access logs. Cross-checked against GitLab Advisory DB and OpenCVE which confirm CWE-639 and the affected route. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/langflow/CVE-2026-55255/ • https://app.opencve.io/cve/?vendor=langflow
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: high — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate Langflow users invoking their own flows via the OpenAI-compatible /api/v1/responses endpoint
  • Application SDKs / agent frameworks calling the endpoint at high volume as designed
  • CI/CD pipelines or synthetic monitors exercising the Langflow API
  • Kubernetes/ELB health-check probes hitting the endpoint
  • Internal integration services that legitimately run shared flows
Suppression filter
| where csUserAgent !has "kube-probe" and csUserAgent !has "ELB-HealthChecker" and csUserAgent !has "Datadog"
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the distinct (csUsername, cIP, csUriStem, scStatus) tuples; flag principals running flows they don't normally run and any source IP outside the known app/integration ranges. 200 responses mean the flow actually executed. 2) Contain: if a non-owner principal received 200s, rotate that user's API key, and upgrade the Langflow instance to >=1.9.1 (CVE-2026-55255 fixed in 1.9.1 / PR #12832) which forces cross-user lookups to 404. 3) Hunt: pivot the same source IP/user across /api/v2/workflow and /api/v1/run*, and review whether sensitive flows (those handling secrets/PII) were among the executed flow IDs.
W3CIISLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where csMethod == "POST"
| where csUriStem has_any ("/api/v1/responses", "/api/v2/workflow") or csUriStem startswith "/api/v1/run"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, SourceIP = cIP, User = csUsername, csMethod, csUriStem, Status = scStatus, csUserAgent
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Langflow IDOR enumeration — single source fan-out to /api/v1/responses (CVE-2026-55255) AIReconnaissance Medium
Detects a single authenticated user or source IP issuing more than 20 POSTs to /api/v1/responses within a 5-minute window — the signature of automated enumeration of victim flow UUIDs to find and execute flows owned by other users, as enabled by CVE-2026-55255. Rationale: Implements the reviewer's >20-requests-in-5m aggregation against the exact IDOR endpoint. Rapid sequential POSTs to /api/v1/responses with varying flow IDs in the body is the only way to brute-force/enumerate other users' flow UUIDs, since the helper leaks no 403-vs-404 oracle before the 1.9.1 fix. Threshold tier because it carries a concrete temporal/volume condition. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/langflow/CVE-2026-55255/
ATT&CKT1190 T1213
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Batch agent applications legitimately running many flow executions per minute
  • Load/performance testing against the Langflow API
  • A shared service account fronting many end users so all volume attributes to one principal/IP
  • Reverse proxy collapsing many clients behind one egress IP (NAT)
  • Scheduled bulk-inference jobs that fire dozens of flow runs together
Suppression filter
| where User !in~ ("svc-langflow-batch") and csUserAgent !has "k6" and csUserAgent !has "Locust"
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm whether the bursting principal/IP is a known batch/service identity; inspect the spread of scStatus — a mix of 404s turning into 200s strongly indicates UUID enumeration finding valid flows. 2) Contain: throttle/block the offending source at the proxy/WAF, disable or rotate the abused API key, and upgrade Langflow to >=1.9.1. 3) Hunt: enumerate which flow IDs returned 200 for this principal and cross-reference flow ownership; check the same source against /api/v2/workflow and /api/v1/run* for parallel enumeration.
W3CIISLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where csMethod == "POST"
| where csUriStem == "/api/v1/responses"
| summarize RequestCount = count(), DistinctStatuses = make_set(scStatus), FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated) by SourceIP = cIP, User = csUsername, bin(TimeGenerated, 5m)
| where RequestCount > 20   // empirical: legit clients rarely exceed ~20 flow executions / 5m; enumeration spikes well above
| order by RequestCount desc

Asset exposure — Langflow instances vulnerable to CVE-2026-55255 (<1.9.1) AIReconnaissance High
Deterministic exposure hunt: enumerates managed hosts whose installed Langflow version is flagged vulnerable to CVE-2026-55255 by the threat-and-vulnerability inventory. Prioritises internet-facing/authenticated instances for upgrade to 1.9.1 before the IDOR can be exploited. Rationale: Pure IOC-driven exposure match on the article's CVE id (CVE-2026-55255) against the real DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities / CIM Vulnerabilities CveId column — zero ambiguity, no behavioural guesswork. Defender query enriches with IsInternetFacing to prioritise the highest-risk Langflow hosts. Confirmed affected range (<1.9.1) and fix (1.9.1) via GitLab Advisory DB. ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities.Vulnerabilities
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Hosts already patched to >=1.9.1 but scanner inventory not yet refreshed (stale snapshot)
  • Langflow Desktop builds vs server builds reported under the same software name
  • Air-gapped/internal-only instances flagged as if internet-facing
  • Decommissioned VMs still present in the inventory
  • Version string parsed from a side-by-side/secondary install rather than the running service
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the reported SoftwareVersion is genuinely <1.9.1 and that the Langflow service is actually running and reachable (prioritise IsInternetFacing == true). 2) Contain: schedule/expedite upgrade to Langflow 1.9.1 (CVE-2026-55255 fix, PR #12832); for instances that cannot be patched immediately, restrict the /api/v1/responses, /api/v2/workflow and /api/v1/run* routes at the proxy to trusted principals. 3) Hunt: for each exposed host, run the UC_1/UC_2 web-log hunts over the exposure window to determine whether the IDOR was already exercised before patching.

Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).

Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Craft CMS web-server process spawning a shell (post-exploit RCE)

Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] Craft CMS session-riding plugin install POST (/admin/actions/plugins/install-plugin)
Craft CMS resource-js endpoint scanning / access spike from external IPs (CVE-2026-55791) AIReconnaissance Medium
Hunts automated probing of the vulnerable /actions/app/resource-js endpoint from internet sources. Legitimate calls to this endpoint come from authenticated Control-Panel sessions (internal/admin IPs, low volume); a burst of external requests indicates mass scanning for CVE-2026-55791 before exploitation. Rationale: Keys on the exact vulnerable endpoint string (/actions/app/resource-js) from the advisory, scoped to external source IPs and a per-IP volume threshold — the endpoint is normally only hit by internal authenticated CP sessions, so external bursts are scanning. Cross-checked CVE-2026-55791 against GitLab GLAD and paired GHSA-95wr-3f2v-v2wh. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/composer/craftcms/cms/CVE-2026-55791/ • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-95wr-3f2v-v2wh
ATT&CKT1595.002 T1190
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • External uptime/synthetic monitors polling the site front-end that incidentally load CP assets
  • CDN or reverse-proxy health checks egressing from a non-RFC1918 edge IP
  • Legitimate headless preview/build services rendering CP pages
  • Security scanner authorised by the org (Qualys/Tenable/Burp) running a baseline scan
  • A genuinely internet-facing CP behind no VPN where an admin works from a home IP

| where SampleUA !has "UptimeRobot" and SampleUA !has "Pingdom" and SampleUA !has "StatusCake"
Response runbook
1) Triage: review the source IP's user-agents and request rate — scanner UAs (python-requests, nuclei, curl) or many endpoints in <1 min confirm probing. Check whether any request returned 200 with application/javascript (successful exploit). 2) Contain: block the source IP at the WAF/edge and confirm Craft is patched to >=4.18 / >=5.10 and trustedHosts is restricted from ['any'] to the canonical host. 3) Hunt: pivot the same src IP across all web logs for follow-on POSTs to /admin/actions/plugins/install-plugin and for outbound SSRF fetches from the web tier.

| where RequestCount > scan_threshold
| order by RequestCount desc

Craft CMS resource-js reflecting external JavaScript payload (SSRF / arbitrary JS injection) AIExploitation Medium
Detects the core CVE-2026-55791 primitive: an external client receives a 200 application/javascript response from /actions/app/resource-js. Because $baseUrl is poisoned via Host/X-Forwarded-Host, the Guzzle client fetches attacker-hosted content and reflects it as valid JavaScript — the artifact that gets cache-poisoned into admin sessions. Rationale: Combines the exact endpoint (/actions/app/resource-js), the forced response Content-Type (application/javascript) and HTTP 200 named in the advisory, scoped to external clients — the precise observable of the reflected SSRF/JS-injection. Body inspection isn't in standard schemas, so this keys on the response-shape primitive instead. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/composer/craftcms/cms/CVE-2026-55791/ • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-95wr-3f2v-v2wh
ATT&CKT1190 T1059.007
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • An internet-facing CP legitimately serving resource-js to remote admins on public ISP IPs
  • Anonymous front-end templates that legitimately include CP-managed JS assets
  • A CDN edge node fronting the origin appearing as the external src in origin logs
  • Penetration test replaying the PoC against a staging host
  • WAF mis-tagging a same-origin asset request as external due to XFF rewriting
Response runbook
1) Triage: inspect HttpHostName for the responding record — if it is not your canonical Craft host the X-Forwarded-Host poisoning succeeded; compare HttpResponseBodyBytes against the deterministic size of Craft's normal generated JS (deviation = injected/SSRF content). 2) Contain: purge the caching layer for the /actions/app/resource-js URI, block the attacker host/IP, patch to >=4.18/>=5.10 and set trustedHosts to the explicit hostname. 3) Hunt: search admin browser sessions for execution of the poisoned cached JS and for the follow-on POST to /admin/actions/plugins/install-plugin.
ImWebSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(24h)
| where Url has "actions/app/resource-js"
| where HttpResponseHeaderContentType has "javascript"
| where tostring(HttpStatusCode) == "200"
| where not(ipv4_is_private(SrcIpAddr))
| project TimeGenerated, SrcIpAddr, DvcHostname, HttpHostName, Url, HttpMethod, HttpStatusCode, HttpResponseBodyBytes, HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent, HttpRequestHeaderReferer
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Craft CMS web tier SSRF outbound fetch correlated with resource-js request AICommand & Control Medium
Detects the SSRF leg of CVE-2026-55791: the Craft web-app process (php-fpm / w3wp / nginx) making an outbound fetch to an external host, and (Sentinel) correlates it within 10 seconds of an external request to /actions/app/resource-js on the same web host — pinning the poisoned request to the attacker-controlled fetch. Rationale: Ties the advisory's Guzzle SSRF fetch (Craft::createGuzzleClient()->get($url) with ALLOW_REDIRECTS=true) to the web-server process making external connections, and the Sentinel variant enforces the 10s temporal join to the poisoned resource-js request — eliminating generic egress noise. Process list reflects Craft's PHP/Nginx/IIS stack. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/composer/craftcms/cms/CVE-2026-55791/ • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-95wr-3f2v-v2wh
ATT&CKT1090 T1071.001
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Craft pulling legitimate remote assets/plugins or update checks (composer, plugin store, license validation)
  • A configured external asset volume (S3/Cloudinary) fetched by the web tier
  • Webhook/integration calls (Stripe, Mailchimp, headless API consumers) from php-fpm
  • CDN origin-pull or image transform service egress
  • SSO/OIDC metadata fetches over HTTPS from the app process
Suppression filter
| where DstHostname !endswith "craftcms.com" and DstHostname !endswith "packagist.org" and DstHostname !endswith "amazonaws.com"
Response runbook
1) Triage: in the Sentinel join, examine DstHostname/DstIpAddr and the resource-js HttpHostName — an unknown external destination reached <10s after the poisoned request is the SSRF target; check it against threat intel. 2) Contain: block egress from the web tier to the destination, purge any cached resource-js response, and apply the patch + restrict trustedHosts. 3) Hunt: enumerate every external destination this web host fetched in the window and look for the same destination across other Craft hosts (campaign spread) and for the install-plugin POST.

Craft CMS session-riding plugin install POST (/admin/actions/plugins/install-plugin) AIActions on Objectives High
Detects POST requests to /admin/actions/plugins/install-plugin — the session-riding RCE payload in CVE-2026-55791 where poisoned JS uses the admin's CSRF token to silently install a plugin. Plugin installation is a rare, high-privilege CP action, so any occurrence warrants triage. Rationale: Uses the exact RCE endpoint named in the advisory (/admin/actions/plugins/install-plugin) with the POST method — the session-riding action step. Plugin installs are infrequent enough to alert on directly and triage by referer/UA/source for legitimacy. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/composer/craftcms/cms/CVE-2026-55791/ • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-95wr-3f2v-v2wh
ATT&CKT1190 T1185
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A genuine administrator installing or updating a plugin via the CP
  • Automated CI/CD or provisioning tooling that installs plugins during deploy
  • Scheduled plugin auto-update jobs issuing the same POST
  • A developer testing plugin installation on a staging instance
  • Migration scripts that re-install plugins after a restore
Suppression filter
| where HttpRequestHeaderReferer !has "/admin"
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the request maps to a known admin action — check SrcIpAddr against the admin allowlist, the referer (should be a CP page, not absent/external), and whether the user actually intended an install. A missing/foreign referer or external source IP indicates session riding. 2) Contain: if unauthorised, disable the installed plugin, revoke/rotate admin sessions and CSRF tokens, isolate the host, and patch to >=4.18/>=5.10. 3) Hunt: correlate the source/host back to a prior resource-js fetch and SSRF egress, and review the web tier for child-process execution (web shell) following the install.

Craft CMS web-server process spawning a shell (post-exploit RCE) AIInstallation High
Detects the RCE outcome of the CVE-2026-55791 chain: the Craft web-server process (w3wp.exe / php-fpm / php-cgi / nginx) spawning a command interpreter or recon/download binary. A web app process launching cmd/powershell/sh is a high-fidelity indicator that the installed malicious plugin executed code. Rationale: Targets the RCE end-state of the advisory's chain (1-Click RCE via plugin install) by alerting on the Craft web stack (w3wp/php-fpm/php-cgi/nginx) spawning interpreters or LOLBins — a behaviour that should essentially never occur on a healthy Craft host. Parent list reflects Craft's PHP/IIS/Nginx deployment. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/composer/craftcms/cms/CVE-2026-55791/ • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-95wr-3f2v-v2wh
ATT&CKT1190 T1505.003 T1059.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A legitimate Craft plugin that shells out (e.g. running an image-processing CLI, ffmpeg, or a backup script) under php-fpm
  • Deployment tooling or composer post-install hooks executing shell commands during a release
  • Server admins running diagnostic commands inside a web container
  • Monitoring agents invoking whoami/curl under the web service account
  • A cron job mis-attributed to the web-server parent in container PID namespaces
Suppression filter
| where ProcessCommandLine !has "composer" and InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "backup"
Response runbook
1) Triage: examine the child ProcessCommandLine — encoded PowerShell, curl/wget to external hosts, whoami, or writes into the webroot confirm malicious execution; benign image/backup tooling is usually identifiable by path and args. 2) Contain: isolate the host, kill the offending process tree, disable/remove the recently installed plugin, and rotate web-service and admin credentials/sessions. 3) Hunt: walk back to the install-plugin POST and the SSRF egress on this host, capture dropped files in the webroot, and sweep other Craft hosts for the same parent→child pattern.

Crossplane: Signature verification TOCTOU allows installing unverified package content via mutable tag

Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix

Actions on Objectives
Likely
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Crossplane package installed by mutable tag instead of immutable digest · [LLM] Crossplane ImageConfig signature-verification policy deleted or weakened

Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Crossplane package installed by mutable tag instead of immutable digest AIInstallation High
Hunts Kubernetes API audit logs for pkg.crossplane.io Provider/Configuration/Function installs whose spec.package references a version tag rather than an @sha256 digest. This is the exact precondition GHSA-wfqx-gjrf-g28r requires: when a package is pulled by tag, a malicious/compromised OCI registry can serve a correctly-signed image for cosign verification and then swap in unsigned content for the actual pull. Rationale: Built directly on the advisory's stated exploit precondition #2 ('Install packages using tag references rather than digests'). The detection keys on the real Crossplane API (pkg.crossplane.io Provider/Configuration/Function, spec.package) and the digest sentinel '@sha256:' that the fix mandates; absence of the digest is exactly what makes an install TOCTOU-exposed. Corroborated against the GHSA, the GitLab advisory mirror, and Crossplane's ImageConfig/package docs. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-wfqx-gjrf-g28r • https://advisories.gitlab.com/golang/github.com/crossplane/crossplane/v2/GHSA-wfqx-gjrf-g28r/ • https://docs.crossplane.io/latest/packages/image-configs/
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate GitOps/Argo/Flux pipelines that pin Crossplane packages to a semantic-version tag (e.g. provider-aws:v1.20.0) rather than a digest — common and benign in many shops
  • Platform-team installs from the org's own trusted internal registry where tag mutability is acceptable
  • Dev/sandbox clusters where engineers iterate on packages by floating tag
  • Initial cluster bootstrap installing first-party Upbound/Crossplane marketplace packages by tag
  • Renovate/Dependabot-style bots bumping a package tag during routine upgrades
Suppression filter
| where not (PackageRef startswith "xpkg.upbound.io/upbound/" or PackageRef startswith "xpkg.crossplane.io/")   // keep third-party / org-uncontrolled registries (advisory precondition #3), drop trusted first-party marketplace
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the offending Provider/Configuration object and its spec.package. Confirm whether the registry host is one the org controls (advisory precondition #3) and whether an ImageConfig with cosign verification matches that image. A tag-pinned package from a third-party registry under cosign verification is the high-risk case. 2) Contain: re-pin the package to the resolved immutable digest (spec.package: repo@sha256:...) and ensure Crossplane is on a fixed build (v2.3.3 / v2.2.3 or later); for vulnerable builds, prefer digest references org-wide until upgraded. 3) Hunt: inventory all pkg.crossplane.io resources still on tags, review the installed package revisions' Verified condition for skipped/incomplete status, and check whether the registry served differing digests for verify vs pull around the install time.
AKSAudit
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where Verb in ("create", "update", "patch")
| extend ObjRef = todynamic(ObjectRef)
| where tostring(ObjRef.apiGroup) == "pkg.crossplane.io"
| where tostring(ObjRef.resource) in ("providers", "configurations", "functions")
| extend ReqObj = todynamic(RequestObject)
| extend PackageRef = tostring(ReqObj.spec.package)
| where isnotempty(PackageRef) and PackageRef !contains "@sha256:"   // not digest-pinned => mutable tag => TOCTOU-exposed
| extend Actor = tostring(todynamic(User).username)
| project TimeGenerated, Actor, Verb, Kind = tostring(ObjRef.resource), ObjectName = tostring(ObjRef.name), PackageRef, UserAgent
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Crossplane Package Installed By Mutable Tag Without Digest Pin
id: 7a3c1e8b-2f4d-4c9a-b6e1-9d2f5a1c3e47
description: Detects creation/update of a Crossplane Provider, Configuration or Function whose spec.package uses a mutable tag rather than an immutable @sha256 digest, the precondition for the GHSA-wfqx-gjrf-g28r signature-verification TOCTOU.
references:
  - https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-wfqx-gjrf-g28r
  - https://docs.crossplane.io/latest/packages/image-configs/
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/06/21

  - attack.t1195.002

  product: kubernetes
  service: audit
detection:
  selection:
    verb:
      - create
      - update
      - patch
    objectRef.apiGroup: 'pkg.crossplane.io'
    objectRef.resource:
      - 'providers'
      - 'configurations'
      - 'functions'

    requestObject.spec.package|contains: '@sha256:'
  condition: selection and not filter_digest
level: low
source:kubernetes.audit @objectRef.apiGroup:pkg.crossplane.io @objectRef.resource:(providers OR configurations OR functions) @verb:(create OR update OR patch) -@requestObject.spec.package:*sha256*
CLOUD
sourcetype="kube:apiserver:audit" objectRef.apiGroup="pkg.crossplane.io" (objectRef.resource="providers" OR objectRef.resource="configurations" OR objectRef.resource="functions") (verb="create" OR verb="update" OR verb="patch")
| rename requestObject.spec.package as package_ref, objectRef.name as object_name, objectRef.resource as kind, user.username as username
| where isnotnull(package_ref) AND NOT match(package_ref, "@sha256:")
| stats min(_time) as firstTime, max(_time) as lastTime, values(username) as username, values(sourceIPs{}) as src_ips by package_ref, kind, object_name
| convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
| sort - lastTime
Crossplane ImageConfig signature-verification policy deleted or weakened AIInstallation Medium
Alerts on delete/update/patch operations against imageconfigs.pkg.crossplane.io in the Kubernetes audit stream. Crossplane enforces cosign signature verification only for images matched by an ImageConfig (spec.verification.cosign); removing or editing that resource silently disables verification, the defense the GHSA-wfqx-gjrf-g28r TOCTOU already partially undermines. Surfaces whether the post-change object still carries a verification block. Rationale: Grounded in the advisory's statement that 'package signature verification [is] configured via the ImageConfig mechanism' and Crossplane docs confirming the real resource (imageconfigs.pkg.crossplane.io, spec.verification.cosign). Detects the defense-evasion precursor — tearing down or weakening the cosign policy — using only verifiable API/resource names (no skipSignatureVerification field exists in the schema, so that reviewer suggestion was dropped as ungrounded). Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-wfqx-gjrf-g28r • https://docs.crossplane.io/latest/packages/image-configs/ • https://docs.upbound.io/providers/signature-verification
ATT&CKT1562.001
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Platform engineers legitimately rotating cosign authorities/keys inside an ImageConfig during key rollover
  • GitOps reconciliation re-applying an unchanged ImageConfig (server-side apply 'update' with no real diff)
  • Decommissioning an ImageConfig for a registry the org no longer uses
  • Migrating verification config between ImageConfig objects (delete old + create new)
  • Cert-manager or admission-controller status writes touching the resource
Suppression filter
| where Verb in ("delete", "deletecollection") or VerificationSpecPresent == false   // alert on removal/teardown of verification; suppress edits that retain a verification block
Response runbook
1) Triage: diff the ImageConfig before/after the operation — confirm whether spec.verification.cosign authorities were removed, loosened, or the whole object deleted, and identify the actor (human vs service account) and source IP. A delete or removal of the verification block by a non-platform identity is high-confidence malicious. 2) Contain: restore the ImageConfig from source-of-truth/GitOps, and pause new package installs (or require digest pins) until verification is re-established; rotate credentials of the actor if unauthorized. 3) Hunt: list any package revisions installed while the ImageConfig was absent/weakened and check their Verified condition; cross-reference with the mutable-tag install detection to find packages that may have been pulled without enforced verification.
AKSAudit
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| extend ObjRef = todynamic(ObjectRef)
| where tostring(ObjRef.apiGroup) == "pkg.crossplane.io"
| where tostring(ObjRef.resource) == "imageconfigs"
| where Verb in ("delete", "deletecollection", "update", "patch")
| extend ReqObj = todynamic(RequestObject)
| extend VerificationSpecPresent = isnotempty(tostring(ReqObj.spec.verification))
| extend Actor = tostring(todynamic(User).username)
| project TimeGenerated, Actor, Verb, ImageConfigName = tostring(ObjRef.name), UserAgent, VerificationSpecPresent
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Anonymous HTTP 200 to DotVVM endpoints (AuthorizeActionFilter bypass exploitation) AIExploitation Low
Hunts successful (HTTP 200) requests to DotVVM-served content (.dothtml views / dotvvm resource routes) that carry no authenticated user, the observable signature of the no-op AuthorizeActionFilter letting unauthenticated callers reach pages it should have rejected with 401/403. Article-specific because it anchors on DotVVM's own request markers rather than a generic protected-path template. Rationale: Anchored on DotVVM's own request markers ('.dothtml', 'dotvvm' route) confirmed via dotvvm.com routing docs, combined with empty csUserName + scStatus 200 — the only behavioural signature the advisory leaves, since the no-op filter manifests as unauthenticated requests succeeding. defender_kql left empty: Microsoft Defender XDR has no IIS HTTP-status/authenticated-user table (DeviceNetworkEvents lacks HTTP status and request user). Cross-checked GitHub Advisory DB and GitLab advisory (CVSS 3.1, CWE-862). Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-c8qj-jx8j-fg2w • https://advisories.gitlab.com/nuget/dotvvm/GHSA-c8qj-jx8j-fg2w/ • https://www.dotvvm.com/docs/2.0/pages/basics-routing
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: high — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Public, intentionally anonymous DotVVM pages (landing/login/marketing .dothtml views legitimately return 200 to anonymous users)
  • Static DotVVM resource handler fetches (CSS/JS bundled by the framework under dotvvm resource routes)
  • Load-balancer / uptime monitoring probes hitting DotVVM routes anonymously
  • Search-engine crawlers indexing public DotVVM content
  • Pre-auth bootstrap GET of a DotVVM SPA shell before the user signs in
Suppression filter
| where csMethod == "POST" or csUriStem !endswith ".css" and csUriStem !endswith ".js" and csUriStem !has "dotvvmResource"
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the target .dothtml route is meant to be protected (was it decorated with AuthorizeActionFilter?) and that the responding app runs a vulnerable DotVVM build (< 4.2.11, 4.3.0-preview01..4.3.14, or 5.0.0-preview01..08). If the page is public by design, close. 2) Contain: deploy the patched DotVVM (4.3.15 / 4.2.11 / 5.0.0-preview09) or swap AuthorizeActionFilter for AuthorizeAttribute per the advisory workaround; front the route with a WAF rule requiring a valid auth cookie. 3) Hunt: pull all anonymous-200 hits to this site for the same cIP set and review whether sensitive data (export/admin) was returned, then check app logs for state-changing POST postbacks executed without a principal.
W3CIISLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where scStatus == 200
// DotVVM framework markers: .dothtml views or the dotvvm resource/postback route
| where csUriStem has "dotvvm" or csUriStem endswith ".dothtml" or csUriQuery has "dotvvm"
// no authenticated principal on the request = anonymous reach to a protected-by-design page
| where isempty(csUserName) or csUserName == "-"
| summarize Requests = count(), Methods = make_set(csMethod, 10), Paths = make_set(csUriStem, 20), FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated) by Computer, cIP, sSiteName
| sort by Requests desc

Vulnerable DotVVM framework version present on host (GHSA-c8qj-jx8j-fg2w) AIExploitation Low
Exposure hunt that surfaces assets running a DotVVM build affected by the no-op AuthorizeActionFilter: any version < 4.2.11, the 4.3.x line below 4.3.15, or 5.0.0 previews 01-08. Concrete because it encodes the exact affected/patched version boundaries from the advisory. Rationale: Encodes the advisory's exact version boundaries — patched at 4.3.15 / 4.2.11 / 5.0.0-preview09 — with explicit handling of the preview-suffix branch so 5.0.0-preview08 is flagged but preview09 is not. Confidence Low because DotVVM is an embedded NuGet dependency and may not always appear in Defender TVM software inventory; where it does, the match is exact (fp low). Versions cross-checked against the GitHub and GitLab advisory entries. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-c8qj-jx8j-fg2w • https://advisories.gitlab.com/nuget/dotvvm/GHSA-c8qj-jx8j-fg2w/
ATT&CKT1190
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A DotVVM app that does not use AuthorizeActionFilter at all (vulnerable build present but feature unused — exposure, not exploitable)
  • Dev/test or internal-only hosts not reachable by untrusted users
  • A 4.3.15+ build whose installer string still carries a pre-release tag that parses to a lower core version
  • Side-by-side install where an old DotVVM copy lingers in a NuGet cache but the running app references the patched assembly
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the flagged SoftwareVersion is the build actually loaded by a running, internet-reachable DotVVM app (not a stale cache copy), and that the app registers AuthorizeActionFilter. 2) Contain: schedule upgrade to 4.3.15 / 4.2.11 / 5.0.0-preview09 (or apply the AuthorizeAttribute workaround) on exposed hosts first. 3) Hunt: pivot each confirmed host into the anonymous-200 IIS hunt (companion UC) to see whether the no-op filter has already been exercised by unauthenticated callers.
DeviceTvmSoftwareInventory
| where SoftwareName has "dotvvm" or SoftwareVendor has "dotvvm" or SoftwareVendor has "riganti"
| extend VerCore = extract(@"^(\d+\.\d+\.\d+)", 1, SoftwareVersion)
| where isnotempty(VerCore)
| extend V = parse_version(VerCore)
| extend PreviewNum = toint(extract(@"preview0*(\d+)", 1, SoftwareVersion))
| where (V < parse_version("4.2.11"))                                                  // whole 4.2.x and earlier line
     or (V >= parse_version("4.3.0") and V < parse_version("4.3.15"))                   // 4.3.0-preview01..4.3.14
     or (V == parse_version("5.0.0") and SoftwareVersion has "preview" and PreviewNum < 9) // 5.0.0-preview01..08
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, DeviceId, SoftwareVendor, SoftwareName, SoftwareVersion
| sort by DeviceName asc

Network-AI sandbox escape: node spawns /bin/sh -c with allowlist prefix + shell metacharacter injection AIExploitation Medium
Hunts the CVE-2026-54051 core exploit: the network-ai SandboxPolicy allowlist glob-matches the whole command string but ShellExecutor runs it via /bin/sh -c, so an allowed prefix (git/npm/node/yarn/pnpm) followed by ;, &&, ||, $() or backticks becomes arbitrary execution. Surfaces a node parent spawning a shell with -c where the command line carries both an allowlisted prefix and an unquoted shell metacharacter. Rationale: Uses the exact mechanism named in the article — node/npm/git/yarn/pnpm allowlist prefixes glob-matched whole-string, executed through /bin/sh -c, plus the article's enumerated metacharacter set (; & | $ ` ( ) < > { }). The node-parent + sh -c + prefix + metachar combination is the article's primary exploit (UC_12_2).
ATT&CKT1059.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate npm lifecycle scripts that chain shell commands with && (e.g. "build && test")
  • CI/CD runners (Jenkins, GitLab runner) invoking node tooling that shells out
  • pm2 / nodemon process managers launching shell wrappers
  • Developer running a node script that calls git with a $(...) subshell
  • Postinstall hooks combining multiple commands with ;
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("npm run","npm exec","yarn run","node_modules/.bin") and ProcessCommandLine has_any ("&&","||") and not ProcessCommandLine has_any ("id;","whoami","/tmp/","curl ","wget ","nc "))
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full ProcessCommandLine and the parent node command line; identify whether the shell payload follows a legitimate allowlist prefix and whether the metacharacter introduces an unrelated command (id, curl, nc) vs a benign build chain. 2) Contain: if the host runs network-ai < 5.9.1 and the injected segment is non-build (recon/network), isolate the host and kill the orchestrator process; capture /proc and any dropped marker files. 3) Hunt: pivot on the same node parent for child recon binaries and outbound connections; check for network-ai version and remove broad wildcard allowlist entries (node */npm *).
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "node"
| where FileName in~ ("sh","bash","dash")
| where ProcessCommandLine has "-c"
| where ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"\b(git|npm|node|yarn|pnpm)\b"
| where ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"[;&|`$(){}<>]"
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ParentProcessName has "node"
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("sh","bash","dash")
| where ActingProcessCommandLine has "-c"
| where ActingProcessCommandLine matches regex @"\b(git|npm|node|yarn|pnpm)\b"
| where ActingProcessCommandLine matches regex @"[;&|`$(){}<>]"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Network-AI CVE-2026-54051 post-exploit: Linux recon utilities spawned from shell child of node AIActions on Objectives Medium
Detects the post-exploitation enumeration that follows a successful CVE-2026-54051 allowlist bypass: a shell (sh/bash/dash) whose parent is node executing reconnaissance binaries. Fires when the orchestrator-spawned shell runs id, whoami, uname, hostname, curl, wget, nc, ncat, python, perl, ssh-keygen or cat. Rationale: Maps the article's post-exploitation profile (id ran via the injected /bin/sh -c in the PoC) and the reviewer-supplied recon binary set, scoped to a node grandparent in Defender/Falcon where InitiatingProcessParentFileName is available. Sentinel ImProcessCreate exposes only parent (sh) so the node grandparent constraint is relaxed there.
ATT&CKT1033 T1082
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Node-based deploy scripts legitimately calling curl/wget to fetch artifacts
  • Healthcheck wrappers running id/whoami/hostname inside containers
  • CI jobs invoking python via a shell step
  • Admin troubleshooting scripts under a node-managed service
  • Container entrypoint shells gathering uname/hostname at startup
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("/healthcheck","/entrypoint","deploy.sh") )
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the grandparent is the network-ai orchestrator node process and the recon command was not part of a known deploy/healthcheck script; review the preceding sh -c command line for an injected metacharacter. 2) Contain: if recon is paired with outbound nc/curl/wget to an untrusted host, isolate the device and snapshot the process tree and any written files. 3) Hunt: search the same host/account for the allowlist-bypass parent event (UC1), outbound C2, ssh-keygen output, and credential file reads; verify network-ai is upgraded to >= 5.9.1.

Foundational vulnerability-exposure hunt: enumerates hosts where the vulnerability-management feed reports CVE-2026-54051 (network-ai < 5.9.1). Used to scope which assets the behavioural detections (UC1–UC3) should be prioritised against and to drive patch/upgrade to 5.9.1. Rationale: Anchors directly on the article's tracked identifier CVE-2026-54051 and the fixed version 5.9.1, giving an exact asset-exposure list to prioritise the behavioural UCs. Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Vulnerable network-ai (<=5.7.1) npm package present in node_modules
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Unauthenticated non-loopback POST /mcp tool invocation against network-ai SSE server
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] network-ai MCP SSE server launched in non-stdio (vulnerable) mode
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Vulnerable network-ai (<=5.7.1) npm package present in node_modules AIDelivery High
Asset-inventory hunt that surfaces hosts with the vulnerable Jovancoding/Network-AI package on disk (CVE-2026-48814 / CVE-2026-46701). The package files land under node_modules\network-ai; presence is the precondition for the unauthenticated MCP SSE exposure. Triage version against the 5.7.2 fixed release. Rationale: Pivots on the concrete install path 'node_modules\network-ai' named in the advisory (bin/mcp-server.ts, lib/mcp-transport-sse.ts live under it). Cache-path exclusions cut npm/pnpm store noise so only real project installs surface for remediation against the 5.7.2 fix. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/network-ai/CVE-2026-46701/ • https://radar.offseq.com/threat/cve-2026-48814-cwe-306-missing-authentication-for--a37c283f4afc7554 • https://www.squaredtech.co/mcp-security-flaw-npm-package-ships-with-no-auth-by-default
ATT&CKT1190 T1195.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer workstation with network-ai intentionally installed for legitimate multi-agent orchestration
  • CI/CD build agent pulling the package during npm/pnpm install for tests
  • Security researcher detonating the package in a sandbox
  • Already-remediated 5.7.2+ install (identical path, patched version — verify package.json version field)
  • Monorepo with nested node_modules copies inflating host count
Suppression filter
| where FolderPath !has @"_cacache" and FolderPath !has @"\.npm\" and FolderPath !has @"\pnpm-store\"
Response runbook
1) Triage: open package.json under the flagged node_modules\network-ai path and read the version; if >=5.7.2 it is patched — close. If <=5.7.1, confirm whether the host runs the SSE MCP server (see companion process UC). 2) Contain: have the owner upgrade to network-ai 5.7.2+, or if the server is running, set a non-empty NETWORK_AI_MCP_SECRET and rebind the listener to 127.0.0.1 until patched. 3) Hunt: pivot the host into the process-launch and inbound-POST UCs to determine whether the vulnerable SSE server was ever started or reached from a non-loopback source.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FolderPath has @"node_modules\network-ai"
| where FolderPath !has @"_cacache" and FolderPath !has @"\.npm\" and FolderPath !has @"\pnpm-store\"
| summarize FirstSeen=min(Timestamp), LastSeen=max(Timestamp), FilesWritten=dcount(FileName), SamplePath=any(FolderPath), SawPackageJson=maxif(1, FileName =~ "package.json") by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by FirstSeen desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where TargetFilePath has @"node_modules\network-ai"
| where TargetFilePath !has @"_cacache" and TargetFilePath !has @"\pnpm-store\"
| summarize FirstSeen=min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen=max(TimeGenerated), FilesWritten=dcount(TargetFileName), SamplePath=any(TargetFilePath) by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName
| order by FirstSeen desc

network-ai MCP SSE server launched in non-stdio (vulnerable) mode AIInstallation High
Detects process creation of the Network-AI MCP server (command line contains both 'network-ai' and 'mcp-server') without the --stdio flag, i.e. the SSE listener that ships with the empty-default-secret authorization bypass. Stdio-mode invocations are excluded because the advisory's only safe path requires --stdio. Rationale: Uses the advisory's exact invocation tokens — the 'network-ai' package and its 'mcp-server' bin — and excludes --stdio, the only mode the advisory deems non-vulnerable. Surfaces the listening SSE server before exploitation rather than a generic node process. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/network-ai/CVE-2026-46701/ • https://www.squaredtech.co/mcp-security-flaw-npm-package-ships-with-no-auth-by-default
ATT&CKT1059 T1133
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Operator running the server locally bound to 127.0.0.1 for legitimate single-user use
  • Developer testing network-ai during feature work
  • Automated integration-test harness spinning the server up and tearing it down
  • Patched 5.7.2+ host where the secret is now enforced (still launches the same process — confirm version)
  • Container entrypoint launching the SSE server with a properly set NETWORK_AI_MCP_SECRET
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the on-disk network-ai version (<=5.7.1 = exposed) and whether NETWORK_AI_MCP_SECRET is set in the service/unit/container env; an empty/unset secret means _isAuthorized() returns true for all callers. 2) Contain: stop the SSE listener or rebind it to loopback, set a strong NETWORK_AI_MCP_SECRET, and upgrade to 5.7.2+. 3) Hunt: pull DeviceNetworkEvents/web logs for this host and run the inbound-POST /mcp UC to see whether any non-loopback caller already reached config_set / agent_spawn / token_* tools.

| order by TimeGenerated desc

Unauthenticated non-loopback POST /mcp tool invocation against network-ai SSE server AIExploitation Medium
Active-exploitation detection for CVE-2026-48814: a non-loopback caller (curl/SSRF/0.0.0.0 bind) issues POST /mcp to the Network-AI SSE server and gets HTTP 200 with no Authorization header, invoking MCP tools (config_set, agent_spawn, blackboard_write, token_*) against the empty default secret. Web/proxy logs carry the path+method+auth-header signal; EDR network telemetry carries the non-loopback inbound exposure. Rationale: Targets the advisory's PoC exactly — POST /mcp, HTTP 200, no auth, non-loopback source — using the web-log auth-header emptiness (ImWebSession.HttpRequestHeaderAuthorization) as the unauthenticated signal, with EDR inbound-non-loopback as a corroborating exposure pivot when proxy logs are absent. Datadog left blank: no generic web-access-log source in this tenant's listed schema carries POST/path/auth-header. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/network-ai/CVE-2026-46701/ • https://radar.offseq.com/threat/cve-2026-48814-cwe-306-missing-authentication-for--a37c283f4afc7554 • https://www.squaredtech.co/mcp-security-flaw-npm-package-ships-with-no-auth-by-default
ATT&CKT1190 T1059
Data sourcesWeb
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Internal vulnerability scanner / ASM probing the /mcp endpoint
  • Authorized local MCP client legitimately sending no Authorization header because the secret was never configured (still the vuln — worth flagging)
  • Reverse-proxy or container health check polling /mcp
  • Container/NAT bridge rewriting a loopback request so it appears as a non-loopback SrcIpAddr
  • Load balancer or service mesh sidecar fronting the SSE server with stripped auth headers
Suppression filter
| where SrcIpAddr !in ("127.0.0.1", "::1")
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the destination host runs network-ai <=5.7.1 SSE mode, then inspect the request URL/body for invoked tool names (config_set, agent_spawn, blackboard_write, token_revoke) and check the source IP against known scanners and internal ranges. 2) Contain: block ingress to the MCP port from non-loopback sources at the host/SG firewall, set a strong NETWORK_AI_MCP_SECRET, restart the service, and upgrade to 5.7.2+. 3) Hunt: enumerate every POST /mcp 200 from this source across all hosts, review token_* and config_set invocations for tampering or stolen credentials, and rotate any tokens the blackboard/agent tools could have exposed.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "network-ai" and InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "mcp-server"
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "--stdio"
| where ActionType == "InboundConnectionAccepted"
| where RemoteIPType != "Loopback" and RemoteIP !in ("127.0.0.1", "::1")
| summarize Connections=count(), FirstSeen=min(Timestamp), LastSeen=max(Timestamp), Ports=make_set(LocalPort, 10) by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemoteIPType, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| order by FirstSeen desc
ImWebSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where HttpMethod =~ "POST"
| where Url has "/mcp"
| where HttpStatusCode == 200
| where isempty(HttpRequestHeaderAuthorization)
| where SrcIpAddr !in ("127.0.0.1", "::1")
| project TimeGenerated, SrcIpAddr, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber, HttpMethod, Url, HttpStatusCode, UserAgent=HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent
| order by TimeGenerated desc

AutoJack: How a single page can RCE the host running your AI agent

Microsoft Security Blog
2026-06-19 8 use cases 7 techniques 5 kill-chain phases detected

AutoJack is a novel exploit chain showing how a single malicious webpage can turn an AI browsing agent into a remote code execution vector on the host machine. By abusing trust in localhost, missing authentication, and unsafe parameter handling, attackers can trigger arbitrary process execution through AutoGen Studio’s MCP WebSocket. The research highlights a broader pattern - when agents can browse untrusted content and access local services, traditional boundaries like localhost are no longer secure. The post AutoJack: How a single page can RCE the host running your AI agent appeared first o…

AutoGen Studio agent (python) spawns shell/LOLBin as 'MCP server' (AutoJack RCE) AIExploitation Medium
Hunts the server-side manifestation of the AutoJack chain: AutoGen Studio's MCP WebSocket decodes an attacker-supplied base64 server_params blob into StdioServerParams and hands command+args to stdio_client, spawning an arbitrary process. The Python host running `autogenstudio` becomes the parent of a shell/LOLBin (e.g. calc.exe, powershell.exe -enc, bash -c) that is never a legitimate stdio MCP server runtime. Rationale: Anchors on the article's exact exploitation primitive — `autogenstudio` hosting `StdioServerParams.command`/`stdio_client` — and the literal demo payloads (calc.exe, powershell.exe -enc, bash -c). Excludes legitimate stdio MCP server runtimes (modelcontextprotocol/mcp-server) so only abnormal shell/LOLBin children fire. Corroborated via Microsoft MSRC blog, BleepingComputer and The Hacker News write-ups of the same fix (commit b047730). Cross-checked against: • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/06/18/autojack-single-page-rce-host-running-ai-agent/ • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/microsoft-fixes-autogen-studio-flaw-that-enabled-code-execution/ • https://thehackernews.com/2026/06/autojack-attack-lets-one-web-page.html
ATT&CKT1059 T1203
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer legitimately configuring a shell-based stdio MCP server (e.g. an MCP server whose command is powershell/bash) inside AutoGen Studio
  • AutoGen demo/tutorial scripts that intentionally launch calc.exe or cmd.exe as an example tool
  • A python-based MCP wrapper invoking certutil/curl for legitimate file retrieval as a tool action
  • Security testing / red-team validation of the AutoJack PoC in a lab
Suppression filter
| where not (ProcessCommandLine has_any ("modelcontextprotocol","mcp-server","@modelcontextprotocol","uvx","npx"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full ParentCommandLine and child ProcessCommandLine. Confirm the parent is an `autogenstudio` host and the child is an interactive shell/LOLBin, not a declared MCP server (no modelcontextprotocol/mcp-server token). Decode any -enc/base64 in the child cmdline. 2) Contain: isolate the host, kill the autogenstudio process tree, and block outbound from the spawned child; preserve the AutoGen session logs. 3) Hunt: pivot on the same host/user for prior external page fetches by the agent immediately before the spawn (UC3), any loopback :8081 MCP connections (UC2), and subsequent persistence or C2 from the spawned shell.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "autogenstudio" or (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "autogen" and InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "mcp")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","bash.exe","sh.exe","wsl.exe","calc.exe","mshta.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe","regsvr32.exe","rundll32.exe")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where not (ProcessCommandLine has_any ("modelcontextprotocol","mcp-server","@modelcontextprotocol"))
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, ChildBinary = FileName, ChildCmd = ProcessCommandLine, FolderPath, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
| where ActingProcessCommandLine has "autogenstudio" or (ActingProcessCommandLine has "autogen" and ActingProcessCommandLine has "mcp")
| where TargetProcessName has_any ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","bash.exe","sh.exe","wsl.exe","calc.exe","mshta.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe","regsvr32.exe","rundll32.exe")
| where not (TargetProcessCommandLine has_any ("modelcontextprotocol","mcp-server","@modelcontextprotocol"))
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetProcessName, TargetProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by TimeGenerated desc

id: 5f3c1a92-7b4e-4d61-9a2f-2c8e6b1d4a07

description: Detects an AutoGen Studio python host spawning a shell or LOLBin via the MCP stdio_client path, the server-side effect of the AutoJack RCE chain.
references:

author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/06/26

  - attack.t1203

  category: process_creation
  product: windows
detection:

    ParentCommandLine|contains: 'autogenstudio'

    Image|endswith:
      - '\cmd.exe'
      - '\powershell.exe'

Loopback WebSocket to AutoGen MCP control plane carrying server_params (AutoJack) AIDelivery Low
Hunts the cross-localhost-boundary step of AutoJack: an agent/python process opening a WebSocket to the AutoGen Studio MCP endpoint on 127.0.0.1:8081 with a `server_params` query string (the base64 StdioServerParams blob). On patched builds server_params is no longer read from the URL, so its presence on the `/api/mcp/ws/` path is a strong vulnerable/exploited signal. Rationale: Anchors on the article's literal endpoint `ws://localhost:8081/api/mcp/ws/?server_params=` and the named browsing tools (MultimodalWebSurfer, Playwright, fetch_webpage_tool). The `server_params` token on the MCP path is high-fidelity because the patched build (commit b047730) removed URL-borne params in favour of a POST + UUID. Telemetry caveat: many EDRs do not reliably log loopback connections or WebSocket URLs, so confidence is Low; the process+port pivot still surfaces candidates. Cross-checked against: • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/06/18/autojack-single-page-rce-host-running-ai-agent/ • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/microsoft-fixes-autogen-studio-flaw-that-enabled-code-execution/
ATT&CKT1190 T1559
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate AutoGen Studio MCP usage that still passes server_params via the URL on an unpatched/older build
  • Local developer testing the MCP WebSocket with curl/wscat against 127.0.0.1:8081
  • Other locally-bound dev tools coincidentally on port 8081
  • Reverse-proxy or sidecar that rewrites loopback MCP URLs
Suppression filter
| where RemoteUrl has "server_params" and not (RemoteUrl has_any ("node","npx","uvx","modelcontextprotocol"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: capture the full RemoteUrl and base64-decode the `server_params` value to reveal the StdioServerParams command/args; benign use will reference a real MCP runtime, malicious use a shell/LOLBin. 2) Contain: if the decoded command is a shell or arbitrary binary, isolate the host and kill the autogenstudio process; upgrade AutoGen Studio to a build at/after commit b047730. 3) Hunt: correlate to UC1 (did a shell actually spawn?) and UC3 (was the agent fetching an external page just before this connection?).
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where RemotePort == 8081
| where RemoteIP in ("127.0.0.1","::1") or RemoteUrl has_any ("127.0.0.1","localhost")
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "autogenstudio" or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("MultimodalWebSurfer","playwright","fetch_webpage") or InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("python.exe","pythonw.exe")
| where RemoteUrl has_any ("server_params","/api/mcp/ws")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, RemoteIP, RemotePort, RemoteUrl
| order by Timestamp desc

| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)

| where DstIpAddr in ("127.0.0.1","::1") or DstHostname in~ ("localhost","127.0.0.1")
| where Url has_any ("server_params","/api/mcp/ws")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, SrcIpAddr, SrcUsername, Url, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber, HttpUserAgent = HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent
| order by TimeGenerated desc

AutoJack confused-deputy: agent fetches external page then hits local MCP :8081 AIDelivery Medium
Hunts the AutoJack sequence where a browsing agent renders an untrusted external page and, within seconds, the same agent process opens a loopback connection to the AutoGen MCP control plane on 127.0.0.1:8081. This external-content-to-localhost pivot by a single agent process is the behavioural core of the confused-deputy that dissolves the loopback trust boundary. Rationale: Targets the article's central claim — an agent steered by external content drives across the localhost boundary into MCP on :8081 — via a per-process temporal join of a public fetch followed within 120s by a loopback :8081 connection by the same agent PID. Sentinel/Datadog/Falcon left blank: the available Sentinel ASIM tables and the Datadog bare-query/FDR shapes can't express the agent-process-anchored, windowed loopback correlation cleanly, and loopback is rarely logged outside MDE DeviceNetworkEvents. Corroborated by the Microsoft MSRC blog and The Hacker News. Cross-checked against: • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/06/18/autojack-single-page-rce-host-running-ai-agent/ • https://thehackernews.com/2026/06/autojack-attack-lets-one-web-page.html
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1185
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A legitimate AutoGen browsing agent that fetches a benign external page and then makes a normal MCP tool call on :8081 in the same task
  • Developer running an agent demo that browses the web and exercises a local MCP server back-to-back
  • Python web scraper unrelated to AutoGen that also happens to talk to a local service on :8081
  • CI/automation harness that fetches external content then triggers a local broker
Suppression filter
| where DelaySec <= 30 and ExternalUrl !has "localhost"
Response runbook
1) Triage: review the ExternalUrl the agent fetched immediately before the loopback :8081 connection and check domain reputation/age; pull the agent's AutoGen session transcript to see what page it was told to render. 2) Contain: if the external page is untrusted/malicious, isolate the host, kill the agent process, and disable the AutoGen MCP WebSocket until upgraded past commit b047730. 3) Hunt: confirm whether a shell/LOLBin actually spawned (UC1) and decode the server_params on the :8081 connection (UC2); sweep other agent hosts for the same external domain.
let Window = 120s;
let Loopback = DeviceNetworkEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(14d)
    | where RemotePort == 8081 and RemoteIP in ("127.0.0.1","::1")
    | where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("python.exe","pythonw.exe") or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "autogenstudio"
    | project LoopbackTime = Timestamp, DeviceId, InitiatingProcessId, AgentCmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine;
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("python.exe","pythonw.exe") or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("autogenstudio","MultimodalWebSurfer","playwright","fetch_webpage")
| join kind=inner Loopback on DeviceId, InitiatingProcessId
| where LoopbackTime between (Timestamp .. Timestamp + Window)
| project ExternalFetchTime = Timestamp, LoopbackTime, DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', LoopbackTime, Timestamp), DeviceName, ExternalIP = RemoteIP, ExternalUrl = RemoteUrl, AgentCmd
| order by ExternalFetchTime desc

Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic where (All_Traffic.app IN ("python.exe","pythonw.exe") OR All_Traffic.process_name IN ("python.exe","pythonw.exe")) by All_Traffic.src All_Traffic.dest All_Traffic.dest_port _time span=2m
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| eval is_loopback=if((dest IN ("127.0.0.1","::1") AND dest_port=8081),1,0)
| eval is_external=if((NOT (cidrmatch("10.0.0.0/8",dest) OR cidrmatch("192.168.0.0/16",dest) OR cidrmatch("172.16.0.0/12",dest) OR cidrmatch("127.0.0.0/8",dest))) AND dest_port!=8081,1,0)
| stats sum(is_loopback) as loopback_hits sum(is_external) as external_hits values(dest) as dests by src _time
| where loopback_hits>0 AND external_hits>0
| sort - _time
| tstats summariesonly=false count from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic where (All_Traffic.app IN ("python.exe","pythonw.exe") OR All_Traffic.process_name IN ("python.exe","pythonw.exe")) by All_Traffic.src All_Traffic.dest All_Traffic.dest_port _time span=2m
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| eval is_loopback=if((dest IN ("127.0.0.1","::1") AND dest_port=8081),1,0)
| eval is_external=if((NOT (cidrmatch("10.0.0.0/8",dest) OR cidrmatch("192.168.0.0/16",dest) OR cidrmatch("172.16.0.0/12",dest) OR cidrmatch("127.0.0.0/8",dest))) AND dest_port!=8081,1,0)
| stats sum(is_loopback) as loopback_hits sum(is_external) as external_hits values(dest) as dests by src _time
| where loopback_hits>0 AND external_hits>0
| sort - _time

      cross-shell escaping
